# Campaign plan for #2997

The plan of [campaign #2999](https://github.com/samchon/automovie/issues/2999) for [#2997](https://github.com/samchon/automovie/issues/2997): every defect and every non-standard choice of the [survey](survey.md), mapped to the change that removes its cause. Step 3 of the Human skill's work order implements it in one pass.

## Goal

From #2997's Done when:

- every f1 module is fitted;
- each fit's surface distance to its observations is recorded;
- the exporter writes the modules, and the generator renders f1 from them without the world-height calibration and its repair rules.

## Shared method

Every module is a construction of few parameters whose form comes from anatomy and mathematics. Observations place and size those parameters and never choose how many there are. The shared viewer judges the result. The owner's orders govern every rule below:

- 2026-10-27: the error against the observed rows may grow, beyond the files' tolerance or without limit; no MetaHuman-style regression.
- 2026-10-28 11:3x: a universal algorithm needs the viewer; whatever was optimised without it is overfitted and is erased.

The form:

- In station: the natural cubic spline through keys at the module's anatomical stations, its joints and its measurement sites. It is the least-curvature interpolant of the keys (Green and Silverman 1994, Nonparametric Regression and Generalized Linear Models, ch. 2), so no wrinkle shorter than the key spacing can appear.
- In phase: a periodic cubic B-spline of N = 16 control points on every key of every module. It is the least count at which each of a section's four anatomical meridians and each quadrant between them has a control of its own (Keys and sections).
- At a key: the section is sized by the module's own observed section in the key's plane, by least squares at the chain's phases. This is skinning through cross sections (Piegl and Tiller 1997, The NURBS Book, 2nd ed., sec. 10.3; Woodward 1988, Skinning techniques for interactive B-spline surface interpolation, Computer-Aided Design 20(8)). Feature-curve mannequins build bodies the same way, from semantic cross sections at the anthropometric sites (Wang 2005, Parameterization and parametric design of mannequins, Computer-Aided Design 37(1); Hsiao and Chen 2013, A study of surface reconstruction for 3D mannequins based on feature curves, Computer-Aided Design 45).
- At an end: the cap's continuation from the end section to the terminal, along the cap formula's circular meridian (Caps).

The observed rows between keys shape nothing. Only the surface distance report reads them, and no rule reads that report.

### Erased and replaced

The previous method fitted every module to all of its observed rows. It chose each count and smoothing parameter by the error on held-out rows. Every rule below was tuned against the rows by numbers alone, and none was judged in a viewer. Each one is overfitting.

The rows are sections of one smooth, densely triangulated mesh, so neighbouring rows predict each other almost exactly. A held-out score over them falls as the model grows until the model reproduces the rows themselves, and it measures reproduction of the mesh, not the anatomy.

| Erased rule | Why it overfits | Replacement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| interior key count and section count N selected by Ruppert's increasing search under the section-blocked held-out score, coordinate by coordinate, up to the data's limits (up to 183 keys and N up to 456 on f1) | The score keeps falling while counts grow, so the counts follow the density of the rows, not the anatomy. | keys at the module's anatomical stations; N = 16 everywhere (Keys and sections) |
| smoothing parameters `lambda_s`, `lambda_t` chosen by section-blocked cross-validation with Powell's and Brent's methods, Gu's performance iteration and the one-standard-error factor | The rows are the reference surface itself. Cross-validation picks the smoothing that best reproduces that surface, which is regression on the MetaHuman mesh (Order 1). | no smoothing parameter: smoothness comes from the representation |
| squared distance minimisation of the whole surface against every Gauss node of every row, alternated with projection under an Armijo safeguard, kept sets and grazing factors frozen per loop | It pulls the surface onto every node, between the keys as much as at them. Its slow, linear creep toward the rows is the symptom: on f1's left big toe the objective was still falling at alternation 464, when the run was stopped (`.shots/claude-scratch/method/exercise-bigtoe_l-sdm5-log.txt`). | each key sized by its own section; no fit of the surface to the rows |
| section control polygons free at every control point and fitted to all rows | The polygon reproduced row detail at up to 456 controls. | 16 controls per key, sized from the key's own section |
| keys spaced evenly by count inside the admissible interval | Placement followed the count search. | keys at anatomical stations; the admissibility test stays, as a stop |
| a station penalty and a harmonic-acceleration phase penalty with null-space coordinates, a cyclic banded Cholesky with a Schur border, and `AutoMovieHumanFeatureChain.weights` | This machinery served only the regression. | small dense least-squares systems (Solves); `weights` is deleted |
| relief nets sized by the count search, heights under two cross-validated difference penalties | Same as the counts and the smoothing. | a net of one control per named landmark in each direction, heights by least squares (Reliefs) |
| trim control count M by the count search, its smoothing cross-validated | Same. | M = N, the ring the trim is paired with (Strips) |
| strip interior rows by the count search with two cross-validated penalties | The rows existed only to chase the strip's observations. | no interior rows: the Hermite blend of the two surfaces' jets |
| opening-margin harmonics H by the count search, smoothing cross-validated | Same. | H = the number of landmarks the part names on the margin (Opening margins) |
| the table of what N = 35 reproduces of each row (`sectionResolution.ts`) | It measured reproduction of the rows. | none |

Kept, because each comes from geometry, anatomy or topology rather than from the rows:

- module frames, anchors, named axes and declared joints;
- the terminal and the caps;
- joins by shared keys, strips and trims;
- the data partition, decided before any surface exists;
- the one-family rule for a key's section;
- the observation families;
- point inversion, which now serves the report, the trims, the reliefs and the strips;
- the retirement of the world-height calibration.

### Representation

The representations the generator evaluates stay as they are:

| Module | Surface | Unknowns |
| --- | --- | --- |
| base or appendage (`IAutoMovieHumanFeatureChain`) | `S(s, t) = O(s) + x(s, t) U(s) + z(s, t) V(s)`; each control coordinate interpolated over the key stations by `AutoMovieHumanFeatureChain.compose` (natural cubic spline, an owner's derivatives at a shared end, the cap formula's planes past `cap.from`), each section a periodic cubic B-spline of N = 16 control points | each key's 16 control points, sized from the key's own section, except a shared key (the owner's) and the pole key (held at the pole) |
| relief (`IAutoMovieHumanRelief`) | base point moved along `axis` by a tensor-product uniform cubic B-spline whose three outer rings are zero | the interior heights, one row per named landmark station and one column per named landmark phase |
| strip (`IAppendage`) | clamped cubic B-spline across, edge rows from both surfaces' jets, no interior rows, periodic cubic B-splines along the loop | the reach |
| trim (`IAppendage.root`) | periodic cubic B-spline of M = N control points in the parent's chart | the M control points |
| opening margin (`IAutoMovieHumanLoftAperture`) | a Fourier series of H harmonics per coordinate in the opening's frame, the record's own form, H the number of landmarks the part names on the margin | the `2H + 1` coefficients per coordinate |
| eye globe (`IEye`) | the biometric globe of `AutoMovieHumanEyeGlobe` | its centre and its seat sphere's radius, by Gauss-Newton and Brent's method, with no penalty and no count (Head, Eye openings) |

Frames, key stations, poles and shared keys are fixed before a fit. With them fixed, a key's section, a relief, a trim and a margin at fixed parameters are linear in their unknowns. The reach and the globe alone enter non-linearly.

- A key's plane is the module's plane function at the key's station: the normal plane of the module's axis curve, turned across a joint as the observation plan turns its planes. That is `planBodySections.ts`'s rule (`:144`-`:148`, `:197`-`:212`), which the planner exports as a function of station, or a part's own axis curve, such as the Trunk's spline through its joints.
- Its in-plane axes are carried key to key by rotation-minimising transport (Wang, Juttler, Zheng and Liu 2008, Computation of rotation minimizing frames) from one start. No key takes the planner's world-forward reference (`planBodySections.ts:144`-`:148`).
  - A chain whose end key is shared starts from that key's frame, copied from its owner, and carries it over its own keys. `compose` interpolates each key's plane as nine numbers (`AutoMovieHumanFeatureChain.ts:203`-`:206`, `:242`-`:244`), so a frame turned at the shared key would be blended across the last interval: on f1 the arm's front carried to the wrist lies 99.57 degrees from the palm's (Arm). This covers the arm at the wrist, the leg at the ankle and the head base at the neck ring.
  - Any other chain starts at its first key with `u` its front axis (Module frames and handles) made normal to the key's plane, so phase 0 lies at the same anatomical side on every module. This is the Hand's and the Foot's rule.
  - A chain sharing both its end keys would have two starts; it stops and is reported.

The fitter's chain is what the generator renders: the report, the trims, the reliefs and the strips evaluate it by `AutoMovieHumanFeatureChain.compose`, with normals and all surface derivatives from `AutoMovieHumanLoftJet.at`, exact for composed chains.

### Module frames and handles

The owner's order (2026-10-28) requires every module to stay transformable: a later pose, stylisation or deformation acts on modules without a refit, and no stored value is a world height, an absolute coordinate, a person-indexed number or a special case of a chain name. The orthodox form is hierarchical modelling. Each part is stored in its own local frame, the frames form the skeleton's hierarchy, and a transform of a frame carries everything stored in it (Hughes, van Dam, McGuire, Sklar, Foley, Feiner and Akeley 2014, Computer Graphics: Principles and Practice, 3rd ed., ch. 6, hierarchical modelling). Skinned characters bind skin to joints in the same way, in each joint's bind space (Magnenat-Thalmann, Laperrière and Thalmann 1988, Joint-dependent local deformations for hand animation and object grasping).

- Anchor: every module whose axis begins at a bind joint has one anchor frame, the bind frame of that joint. A module with no bind joint has a child frame instead (Child frames below).
  - The axis is the module's axis curve through bind joints. It is the joints' polyline of the observation plan as `planBodySections.ts` builds it, or a part's own curve through the same joints, such as the Trunk's natural spline from `pelvis`, the skeleton's root. A limb's runs from `upperarm` or `thigh`, the palm's from `hand`, the foot's from `foot`, and a digit's or toe's from its `_01`. The Head names its axis joints in its own section.
  - The frame is that joint's bind affine as `body-observations.json` records it (`skeleton.joints[].bindNativeAffineColumnMajor16`), registered by the same axis map as the joint positions (`HumanBodySkeleton.ts:4`-`:11`).
  - Its rotation is taken as the orthogonal factor of the polar decomposition (Higham 1986, Computing the polar decomposition, with applications), so any scale in the record leaves the frame orthonormal; its origin is the joint's bind translation.
  - No anchor is chosen per person or per module by hand. The rule reads the skeleton the source already carries.
- Named axes: a bind frame's axes follow the rig's convention for each bone, so they name no anatomical direction. Each module therefore also declares three unit axes in its anchor's coordinates, built by cross products and Gram-Schmidt from bind joint centres (or from the sources listed below where the joints give one direction), as the ISB segment coordinate systems are built from landmarks (Wu et al. 2002, ISB recommendation on definitions of joint coordinate systems of various joints for the reporting of human joint motion, part I: ankle, hip, and spine, Journal of Biomechanics 35(4); Wu et al. 2005, part II: shoulder, elbow, wrist and hand, Journal of Biomechanics 38(5)):
  - along the module, its axis curve's tangent at the anchor;
  - toward its front: dorsal for a hand, a foot, a digit or a toe, forward for the trunk and the head;
  - lateral, the cross product of the other two.
  - Which source gives the second direction, in this order:
    - the module's own joints, where they span two directions: a limb's hinge, the Arm's elbow, the Leg's and the Foot's ankle bend axis, the Trunk's hip axis, the Hand's knuckle line;
    - its parent's axes carried onto its own axis, where its joints give one direction only and it has a parent: the digits and the toes;
    - landmarks or courses, where its joints give one direction and it has no parent axis to carry: the head base's front from the Frankfurt points (Head). Order 2 counts landmark centres among the centres a general rule derives.
  - The trunk's forward carried onto the femur lies 8.32 degrees off the leg's hinge-normal front on f1 (Leg), which is why a limb builds its own.
  - "Up", "forward", "dorsal" and "palmar" are then a module's own directions, and they turn with it (Integration, "Frames, anchors and joints").
- Named frames: a module may store further frames in its anchor's coordinates, each under a name, where its rule needs one: the head base's `frankfurt`, its anatomical horizontal for gaze and the head's consumers (Head).
- Child frames: a module with no bind joint has a child frame of its parent's anchor instead of an anchor of its own: the auricles, the eye openings with their globes, and the nostril openings with their pockets.
  - Its origin and named axes come from the module's own rule, such as a root loop's centroid or a globe's centre, and are stored in the parent anchor's coordinates.
  - `compose` and `merge` place it through the parent's anchor, so a transform of the parent carries it, and a transform of the child frame moves, scales or turns the module about its own centre.
- Declared joints: each module declares the bind joints inside it or at its ends by their source names, each with its point in the anchor's coordinates and its station where the joint's plane lies in the module's station range. Each joint is declared by one module only.
  - The station range runs from the first key to `cap.from`, where the module's planes are its plane function's. Past `cap.from` the planes are the cap formula's, whose station is no arc length along the axis, so a joint there carries its point only (Foot).
- Stored relative to the anchor:
  - a key's station is arc length along the module's axis from the anchor joint, never a world height;
  - a key's plane (`frame`) is given in the anchor's coordinates, and every key carries one;
  - a cap's pole and a relief's axis are in the anchor's coordinates.
  - A relief's window is stations and phases of its base, and a trim's root is stations and phases of its parent (`IAppendage.root`), so both are already relative to the modules they ride on.
  - Observations, fits and the surface distance work in the registered world frame. The fit's output is converted to the anchor's frame by the anchor's rigid transform, which changes no fitted shape.
- Composition: interpolation commutes with the anchor's map. The natural spline and the B-spline sections are affine combinations of their keys and control points, their weights summing to one, so composing a module in its own frame and then placing it by its anchor equals composing it in place (the affine invariance of Bézier and B-spline curves: Farin 2002, Curves and Surfaces for CAGD, 5th ed.). `compose` places each module's keys by its anchor, then copies an owner's placed end layer into a shared slot unchanged. The copy is bit for bit, and the inherited-phase rule of the Integration section relies on that.
- Handles, what a module exposes to a later transform:
  - the anchor, a rigid frame: placing, posing or scaling the whole module about its centre;
  - the key stations: lengthening or shortening along the axis, each key an anatomical site;
  - each key's control polygon in its plane: section shape and size, the stylisation of a cross-section. Its four meridian controls carry the breadth and depth that anthropometry measures at the site, and its four quadrant controls carry the fullness between them (Keys and sections);
  - the cap's pole: tip length and position;
  - a relief's net and window: the feature's projection and extent, one height per named landmark;
  - a strip's reach: the softness of a join;
  - a trim's control points: the outline of a root.
  - Strips are rebuilt from both surfaces' jets and trims live in the parent's chart, so a join follows its modules wherever their anchors take them. The anchors are joints of one skeleton, so a pose of the skeleton moves every module rigidly. This campaign stores the form and builds no posing.
- Interface: `IAutoMovieHumanFeatureChain` today stores world coordinates. A key's station is "the world height of a horizontal one" (`IAutoMovieHumanFeatureChain.ts:91`-`:94`), its absent `frame` means horizontal at that height (`:96`-`:100`), the pole is in world mm (`:85`), and a relief's axis is a "unit world direction" (`IAutoMovieHumanRelief.ts:33`-`:34`). Files adds:
  - `anchor: { joint: string; frame: AutoMovieHumanLoftFrame.IFrame; axes: { along; front; lateral }; frames?: Record<string, AutoMovieHumanLoftFrame.IFrame>; joints: { name; point; station? }[] }`: the joint's name, its registered bind frame in world mm, the named axes, the named frames and the declared joints in the anchor's coordinates;
  - for a child module, `anchor: { parent: string; frame; axes }` in place of `joint`: the parent chain's name and the child frame and axes in the parent anchor's coordinates;
  - `IAutoMovieHumanLoftAperture` and `IAutoMovieHumanBodyCalibration.IEye` gain `chain`, the chain whose anchor they hang from, and `frame`, their child frame in that anchor's coordinates. The outline's projection axes and the globe's up then come from the module, not world up (Head, Integration). `IAutoMovieHumanHermiteCurve` already names its `chain` (`IAutoMovieHumanHermiteCurve.ts:6`-`:7`), which serves the lip boundary;
  - for an anchored chain, `frame` required on every key, and station, key frames, pole and relief axes read in the anchor's frame.
  - The exporter writes the anchor and the local values (Integration).

### Keys and sections

**Key stations.** A module's keys lie at its anatomical stations, each in the plane its plane function gives there (Representation):

- its first station: the first key past its root, or its shared end key;
- each bind joint it declares inside its station range (Module frames and handles, Declared joints), at the joint's station;
- each measurement site its part names from a published definition, at the station the definition gives. The definitions are ISO 8559-1:2017 (Size designation of clothes, Part 1: Anthropometric definitions for body measurement) and ANSUR II (Gordon et al. 2014) for the body, and Farkas (1994, Anthropometry of the Head and Face) for the head. Where a definition is an extreme, such as the calf girth's maximum or the ankle girth's minimum, the site lies at that extreme of the girths of the module's own observed sections, at the vertex of the parabola through the extreme section and its two neighbours (successive parabolic interpolation: Brent 1973, ch. 5). Observations place the site, and the definition decides that it exists;
- its last station: `cap.from` on a capped end (Caps), or its shared end key;
- on a capped end, the cap keys (Caps).

Two named stations at one station are one key. Nothing else adds, moves or removes a key: no key comes from spacing, from a count, or from a row the surface misses. Measurement sites are the stations #2998's measurement law acts on, so each key is a handle the law and a stylisation can move (Order 2).

A key whose plane meets another key's section inside the skin would fold the surface between them. Each pair of keys is tested (`HumanModuleKeys.admissible`): the line the two planes share must miss the inside of both keys' own outlines, and parallel planes always pass. A failed pair stops the fit and names both stations, so the part corrects its station list. The test moves no key.

**Section count.** N = 16 on every key of every module. The derivation:

- A section's phase starts at the module's front (Representation), so its outline has four anatomical meridians: front, lateral, back and medial, the module's named axes. Anthropometry measures a section by its breadth and depth between those meridians and by its girth.
- A control point of a uniform periodic cubic B-spline acts on 4 of its N spans (local support: de Boor 1978, A Practical Guide to Splines, ch. IX).
- Each meridian's extent is a handle of its own only when its control's support does not reach the next meridian's control. Each quadrant's fullness is a handle of its own only when the quadrant holds a control whose support lies wholly inside it. Both need at least 4 spans per quadrant, so N ≥ 16. The least such N is taken, the fewest parameters.
- One N for every module keeps every shared key compatible, since `compose` refuses a shared key of another control count (`AutoMovieHumanFeatureChain.ts:225`). It lets a strip pair rings of equal count, and gives every section the same handles for a stylisation (Order 2).

Detail finer than a quadrant's fullness is no base skin. It is a relief (Reliefs) or an appendage, by the part's partition.

**Sizing a key.** A key's 16 control points come from its own observed section, the section in the key's own plane (Observation families, Key planes):

1. Take the module's own nodes of that section, by the part's partition, in curve order. Pieces of several curves go round the section's area centroid by angle. Each gap the partition leaves is closed by the chord across it, sampled by 4-point Gauss-Legendre nodes on as many spans as the section's mean own span length gives the chord.
2. Write the outline in the key's in-plane axes and give each point its phase by the chain's correspondence rule (`AutoMovieHumanLoftParameter.phases`), the outline ordered from the front toward +z.
3. The control points minimise `sum_i w_i |C(t_i) - p_i|^2`, `C` the periodic cubic B-spline of 16 control points and `w_i` each point's arc-length element (Gauss weight times speed, or the chord's share). This is least-squares curve approximation at prescribed parameters (Piegl and Tiller 1997, sec. 9.4.1). The normal matrix is the 16 by 16 Gram matrix of the basis at the points, one for both coordinates. Dense Cholesky solves it (Solves), and a non-positive pivot stops the fit, naming the control whose phase interval the outline leaves empty.

Only nodes of the module's own plane-function family serve, and only those of the key's own plane. Another family's sections that the partition gives the module have no plane of the module's keys: on f1, the thumb's oblique sections inside the palm's range (Hand). The surface distance report reads them.

A shared key is the owner's composed end layer, copied. The pole key is held at the pole (Caps).

### Caps

A cap continues its chain past its end key to the pole. The end key's station `cap.from` is given by the part's geometry, by one of two rules that depend on the module's terminal (below):

- When the terminal is a saddle, `cap.from` is the last station whose section is wholly the module's own skin, by the part's partition.
- When the terminal is a maximum, `cap.from` is where, along the module's last run of wholly own sections (the run that reaches the terminal), the section's equivalent radius `r(s) = sqrt(A(s) / pi)` first reaches the pole's height over the section's plane, `rho(s) = (pole - origin(s)) . n(s)`, `n` the plane's normal toward the pole: the zero of `g(s) = r(s) - rho(s)`, by the secant between the last observed plane with `g < 0` and the first with `g >= 0`. The end key need not lie on a plane of an observed family; its plane is the plane function's there, observed on its own (Observation families, Key planes). When `g >= 0` already at the first key, the whole module is its cap.
  - Derivation. The cap formula places its planes at `theta = pi / 2 - sigma (s - s_e) / rho`, its origins at `rho cos(theta)` past the end plane: the planes of a circular meridian of radius `rho` traversed at unit speed in station. The base's unit-speed planes keep the station the arc length of a meridian that runs along the axis. The cap keeps it the meridian's arc length too, and its closure meets the end section with the end section's own extent, exactly when the closure's radial semi-axis equals its axial one: `r(cap.from) = rho`. A section that is not round takes the radius of the disc of its area. Near a non-degenerate maximum a section's size goes as the square root of the remaining height (the Morse lemma: Milnor 1963), which a circular meridian follows and a base's unit-speed planes cannot.
  - Existence and stability. Far from the terminal `rho` exceeds `r` (`g < 0`); at the terminal `rho = 0 <= r`, so a zero exists. `g` grows at `1 + r'(s)`, positive wherever the section shrinks more slowly than the station advances, so the zero is crossed transversally, and a noise `dA` in one section's area moves it by `dA / (2 pi r g')` (0.1 mm² on a 5 mm digit with `g'` 0.7: 0.005 mm). The rule it replaces, the last local maximum of section area, picked a noise maximum: on f1 the index gave 0.000 mm left and 43.837 mm right, the middle 14.000 and 49.013 mm, where the right middle's planes 36 and 37 differ by 0.1 mm² (Hand: `.shots/claude-scratch/hand/geometry-2.txt`).
  - On f1 every digit's and toe's `g` changes sign once along its run, with `g'` from 0.57 to 0.87 at the zero, and left and right agree within 0.04 mm: run length / `cap.from` / `r = rho`, in mm, left then right: thumb 49.848 / 44.133, 44.124 / 5.840, 5.848; index 65.630 / 61.362, 61.334 / 4.870, 4.898; middle 69.013 / 65.658, 65.643 / 5.262, 5.277; ring 60.557 / 57.196, 57.184 / 5.005, 5.017; pinky 48.344 / 44.397, 44.393 / 4.206, 4.210; big toe 32.761 / 23.733, 23.706 / 10.149, 10.176; second toe 28.937 / 22.929, 22.938 / 7.177, 7.169; third toe 23.323 / 17.935, 17.900 / 6.034, 6.069; fourth toe 18.000 / 14.298, 14.321 / 5.406, 5.383; little toe 15.641 / 10.804, 10.780 / 4.870, 4.893 (stations from the run's first plane along its plane origins). Printed by `node --max-old-space-size=4096 --experimental-transform-types --no-warnings --import ./.shots/claude-scratch/hooks.ts .shots/claude-scratch/method/measureCapFrom.ts test/.shots/f1 .shots/claude-scratch/method/capfrom-1.txt`, output `.shots/claude-scratch/method/capfrom-1.txt`. The head's and the auricles' wait on their families (Order of the runs, item 3).
  - The saddle rule alone would put the head's end key at its crown, since every head section from the eyes' tops up is the head's own (Head). The run's last plane would do the same to a digit, a toe or an auricle, whose runs are their own skin to the tip.
  - The rule holds for every module with a maximum terminal: the head base, the digits, the toes and the auricles. `HumanModuleKeys.capFrom` computes it from the run's areas and heights, so every part calls the one rule.

The pole is the module's terminal: the first critical point of the section coordinate on the module's source component past its end key's plane. The coordinate increases along the chain's end direction. Morse theory places a change of the sections' topology only at a critical point of the coordinate (Milnor 1963, Morse Theory, Annals of Mathematics Studies 51; Reeb 1946, Sur les points singuliers d'une forme de Pfaff complètement intégrable ou d'une fonction numérique), so the terminal is either:

- a maximum, where the component ends: a crown, a fingertip or a toe tip;
- a saddle, where it splits into the appendages beyond: the crotch below the trunk, the first web beyond a palm or a foot.

Recovery's section API observes it from the source mesh: seeded with the module's loop in its end key's plane, it follows that loop's component toward increasing coordinates and returns the component's maximum and its critical events with their component counts (`terminalSeeds`, `terminals`, `packages/recovery/src/structures/mesh/IAutoMovieRecoverySectionsInput.ts:35`-`:36`, `IAutoMovieRecoverySourceTerminal.ts`).

- The pole is the first event that splits the component (more of the component's section loops above it than below, counted in the planes through the event's lower and upper regular levels, Recovery's `beforeMm` and `afterMm`; a loop is the component's when its distal skin lies in the component past the end plane) into branches that other modules own, by the part's partition (their skin components): the webs where the digits or the toes part, the crotch where the legs do. The terminal record names each split's owners. An owner's skin is identified by a rule every appendage has, its separated root (`-root-v3.json`): the distal skin of its root loop, flooded from the triangles the loop's cut crosses through triangles wholly past the root plane, as Succession floods a split. A terminal maximum would not do, since a leg ends at its shared ankle key with none. Each loop of the plane through the event's upper regular level (Recovery's `afterMm`) has its distal skin flooded the same way, and owns the modules whose root skin shares a triangle with it (`observeBodySections.ts --owners name=<root file>,...`). The palm's owners are the five digits, the foot's the five toes, the trunk's the two legs, each by its root file. Recovery's `lowerComponents` and `upperComponents` count the components of the critical vertex's lower and upper link, two of each at any saddle, whether the sections split or merge there, so they do not decide a split: on f1 the left palm's terminal reports 11 events, each 2 / 2 or 1 / 0, none with more above than below (`test/.shots/f1/body-sections/palm_l-terminal-v1.json`). A split whose branches all hold the module's own skin, such as a curl of the helix parting from an auricle (Head), is no pole; the run follows the branch that holds the maximum (Observation families, Succession). Without such a split before the maximum, the pole is the maximum, when Recovery marks it single-tip qualified.
- A terminal that is neither stops the fit and is reported, with no fallback.
- One rule holds for every module, and no tie can occur. A farthest observed point would lie in the end plane itself for a module whose observations lie in planes parallel to its end key (the digits and toes, 0.0 mm past it on every digit: survey, Hand), and would tie across the trunk's horizontal lowest row.
- A bottom cap's coordinate increases downward. Its end plane is observed with its normal reversed for the terminal (Observation families below).
- The Trunk asked for its pole where its axis, continued past the end, meets the Rise course. That is rejected. The point depends on how the axis is continued past its last joint and on a measurement course, both constructions. The crotch saddle is the skin's own critical point, where the trunk's component splits into the legs, and Recovery observes it. The same terminal also replaces the Hand's and the Foot's farthest own-skin poles of the palm and the foot base, so every cap has one rule.

Its keys continue the base's mean key spacing `D`: `round(rho pi / (2 D))` keys, at least two, evenly to the pole's station `cap.from + sigma rho pi / 2` (sigma 1 for a top cap, -1 for a bottom one), `rho` the pole's distance from the end plane. The last is the pole key, so at least one cap key lies inside the closure. With none, nothing observed shapes the closure, and the natural spline carries the end section's coefficients to the pole almost linearly in station. The closure is then thinner than the circular meridian the `cap.from` rule is derived for: the viewer showed f1's left big toe, whose `D` gave one key, closing as an ogive (Self-review, "Shared method, views", v08). When `cap.from` is the module's first key, as on an auricle whose section shrinks from its root (Head), no base span gives `D`: the whole module is its cap, and its cap keys lie at the anatomical stations its part names along the cap's meridian, the cap formula's planes there, with the pole's station last. The cap keys' planes are the cap formula's (`compose`), parallel to the end key's plane. Each cap key is sized by its own observed section in its plane, like every key (Keys and sections), and the pole key is held. Between the cap keys the natural spline continues the sections to the pole. No section is scaled by `sin(theta)` and no hemisphere is imposed. A cap plane that holds no loop of the module stops the fit. The terminal replaces every other pole rule of the present fitters and the parts' plans:

- the palm's and foot's middle digit first-key centroid (`fitBodyBase.ts:211`-`:215`);
- the digits' farthest point along the last bone (`fitAppendage.ts:150`-`:163`);
- the trunk's Rise course lowest point (`fitTrunkBase.ts:129`-`:130`), whose role the crotch saddle takes;
- the head's calibration crown (`fitHeadBase.ts:154`).

### Observations and weights

- Each observed loop of the module contributes its Hermite spans at 4 Gauss-Legendre nodes per span, the rule exact to degree 7, which covers a cubic residual squared in the span parameter (Davis and Rabinowitz 1984, Methods of Numerical Integration).
- Each section's open pieces (`report.open`), where the skin it cuts ends at an opening or a contact, contribute their spans as its closed loops do (Head: the head rows are open at the eyes, nostrils and lip contact).
- A node of a key's own section weighs its arc-length element, the Gauss weight times the span's speed and phase interval (Keys and sections).
- Where a sum runs over many sections (the surface distance, a relief's heights, a strip's reach), a node's weight is the surface element it stands for, so the sum approximates the surface integral of the squared distance and dense rows do not outweigh sparse ones. It is the product of:
  - the node's Gauss weight;
  - the span's speed there;
  - the span's phase interval, its arc length share;
  - the section's trapezoid spacing, half the sum of the coordinate gaps to its neighbours among the sections of its own family that the module or strip holds, ordered by coordinate. A section that is the only one of its family the module or strip holds has no such neighbour, and the trapezoid rule gives it no weight; it stands for its own slab of the family, half the sum of its gaps to the family's neighbouring planes, held or not (a one-node midpoint rule over the section's cell along the coordinate: Davis and Rabinowitz 1984). On f1 that is every toe strip's root plane, the foot's and the palm's heads planes and the Leg strip's plane 24 (Foot);
  - over `|grad_S h|`, the surface gradient of the section coordinate `h` at the node's foot. This is the sine of the angle between the section plane and the skin, from the exact jet normal.
  - By the coarea formula the area between two sections `dh` apart along an arc `dl` is `dl dh / |grad_S h|` (Federer 1959, Curvature measures). It equals the plain spacing where the plane crosses the skin at right angles, and grows where the plane grazes it (the head's submental skin, 3.4 degrees on f1, Head).
  - The factor is taken at the node's foot on the surface the sum reads (the final chain for the distance, the base for a relief, the straight blend for a strip). A node whose plane is tangent to the skin at its foot, where the factor has no value, is dropped and counted (reason: plane tangent to the skin).
- A strip or module observed by two families (the arm strip's trunk rows and arm planes) takes each node's spacing within its own family. The partition gives each piece of skin to one family, so the families' parts of the integral are disjoint and add (Arm, Leg).
- Which observations belong to a module is the part's data partition: families, family planes, root loops, landmark regions, apertures, all decided by geometry or topology before the fit.
  - `HumanObservation.ts` carries it as one label per node, the module or "none", given by the part's predicates over observation space: family, plane, a node's position, its arc of a row.
  - A row is cut into arcs where it crosses a plane the part names (a root loop's plane, an armscye plane). The crossings are found by intersection with no tolerance, as the survey's Trunk subsection finds them, and an arc takes one label.
  - The labels are fixed before any surface exists and read no residual.
  - The partition record (each module's node count, every cut and every predicate's inputs) is written with the fit.
  - A node a fit cannot use is dropped and counted by reason. The reasons are those of Foot points, plus "relief region outside its window" for a relief's node whose foot falls outside the window, and "plane tangent to the skin" (above).
- Head sections that follow the lip contact into the mouth or run into a nostril (`EarSectionGeometry.ts:244`-`:255` describes them) are the Head partition's to remove. No observation is ever removed by its residual: no fit has a robust weight. Tukey's biweight and asymmetric least squares are standard M-estimators, but they drop observations whose residual is large, which is the exclusion the campaign forbids, and a misfit they hide is a misfit the surface distance must show.
- A base under a relief is built first, without the observations of the relief's region: each key's section closes the region's gap by its chord, and the natural spline bridges it in station. The relief is then fitted over that base to the observations of its region whose foot falls in its window (Reliefs).

### Observation families

The shared observation code (`observeBodySections.ts`, `findRootKeys.ts`) is Method's.

**Succession.** A family follows its module by topological succession of sections instead of the origin test of `--around` (`observeBodySections.ts:20`-`:23`, `:211`):

- The first plane keeps the loop that winds round its origin, or the loop its part's geometry names where the module's sections need not hold their plane's origin (the auricle's root, found by the Head's ascent from the ear landmarks).
- Each later plane keeps the loop that winds round its origin when that loop overlaps the predecessor's kept loop. Otherwise it keeps the one loop that overlaps the predecessor's kept loop.
- Two loops overlap when a point of either lies inside the other by the even-odd rule, the predecessor's loop projected onto the plane along the plane's normal.
- A plane where no loop overlaps ends the run. Where several overlap and none winds round the origin, the section has split: the run keeps the loop whose distal skin, the exterior reached from it through triangles wholly past its plane, holds the maximum of the module's skin component, the branch of the component's Reeb graph that leads to the terminal (Reeb 1946). The others are recorded and their nodes counted and left out. A split in a module with no skin component (a limb, whose run ends at a shared key), or where no loop's distal skin holds that maximum, ends the run, and is reported.
- Before an appendage's root the loop round the origin is the joined loop both tests keep, so the planes up to the root are unchanged.

**Continuation.** The plan's planes end one bone length past the last joint (`planBodySections.ts:119`-`:126`). The family continues past them:

- Recovery's terminal is seeded with the run's last loop.
- When its maximum lies past the last plane, planes are added along the last plane's normal at the family's own spacing up to the maximum's coordinate and observed with succession. The spacing is the distance between its last two planes.
- The terminal record is saved with the family.
- Each capped module's end key plane, at `cap.from` as its part's geometry gives it, is observed on its own with the axis along the plane's outward normal, and the module's loop in it seeds the terminal: the loop round the module's axis, or the run's own loop where the sections need not hold the axis (an auricle's). The plane need not belong to an observed family. Recovery follows a seed toward increasing coordinates, so a bottom cap's plane takes its normal reversed.

**Key planes.** Every key is sized by the section in its own plane (Keys and sections), and a joint's, a site's or a cap key's plane is in general no plane of an observed family:

- Each module's key planes are observed on their own, as an explicit plane list (`observeBodySections.ts --planes`), into `<module>-keys-v1.json` beside its family.
- Each key section records all its loops. The module's loop is the one that overlaps the module's run loop at the family plane nearest it, by the succession rule's overlap test, and the part's partition labels its nodes by the same predicates as the family's.
- A key plane with no overlapping loop stops and is reported.
- The cap keys' planes follow from `cap.from`, the pole and `D` (Caps), all fixed before a fit, so they are observed with the base keys' planes.

**Families.** The digit and toe families become `<digit>_<s>-v3.json.gz` and `<toe>_<s>-v3.json.gz` (schema `automovie-human-body-sections-v3`), with their terminals. This is the one change both the Hand and the Foot asked for. Every other capped module's family keeps its file, except the head's and the auricles', which are new (below), and its terminal record is written beside it.

**Head families.** Observed for the Head from the planes, axes and selections `HeadGeometry.ts` names:

- `head-v1.json`: planes normal to the head axis at the head families' 0.5 mm spacing, from the Neck Base course's lowest point to the last plane that holds a loop, cut from the head mesh's exterior selection and the vermilion triangles only, so its rows end as open pieces at the openings.
- `auricle_<s>-v1.json`: planes normal to the auricle's signed lateral axis, 0.5 mm apart, from the first plane holding a closed loop that encloses the ear landmarks' centroid seen along the axis out to the last plane that holds a loop, with the root `HeadGeometry.ts` finds by ascent, succession from it and the terminal.
- Each family records every plane's loops with their nesting (a loop inside another is a hole of the section) and the events between planes with their component counts, which the Head reads for the root and the concha.

`observeBodySections.ts` gains a triangle selection, so a family can be cut from named sheets of the source mesh rather than from the whole joined mesh.

**Order of the runs** (Retirement step 1):

1. the `-v3` digit and toe families and every `-root-v3.json`;
2. the digits' and toes' terminals from their runs' last loops;
3. the head and auricle families;
4. the end-plane terminals of the palm, the foot base, the trunk and the head base, each from the plane its part's geometry names once the roots, runs and families it reads exist. The palm needs the digits' roots and terminals, the foot the toes', the trunk the legs' roots, and the head the auricles' roots and families (Hand, Foot, Trunk, Head). The palm's plane is `HandGeometry.read(dir, side).palm.capFrom.plane`, observed along its normal (not reversed) into `palm_<s>-terminal-v1.json`, with the five digits as owners by their root files. The foot's plane is `FootGeometry.read(dir, side).foot.frame(foot.capFrom.station)` (plane 247 on f1), observed along its normal into `foot_<s>-terminal-v1.json`, with the five toes as owners. The trunk's is the Trunk's `trunk-cap-plane.json`, observed reversed into `trunk-terminal-v1.json`, with the two legs as owners.
5. every module's key planes (Key planes), once its part names its stations and its terminal fixes its cap keys.

**Roots.** `findRootKeys.ts` tests the joined loop against every sibling: a loop is joined while it winds round the crossing of its parent's axis or of any sibling appendage's bone polyline. The siblings are the other digits of the palm, the other toes of the foot, and the other limbs of the trunk. This is the separated root of the Hand plan; `measureIdentity.ts` already tests a leg root against the other femur. Its `-root-v2` files hold a neighbour's skin for the left middle, the left pinky and the right ring (survey, Hand). It writes `-root-v3.json` from the `-v3` families, and for the arms and legs from their families as they stand (`arm_<s>-v2.json.gz`, `leg_<s>-v2.json.gz`), whose siblings are the other limbs' bone polylines, continued at both ends (Arm, Leg). The parts and the Trunk's cuts read their roots from those files.

The outputs are private observations under `test/.shots/<alias>/body-sections/`.

### Strips

The strip method (`HumanStripFitting.ts`, driven by `fitStrip.ts`) reads its inputs from a `strips` record, never the parent fit's `regions` and `radius`. The part that defines the trim writes the record from its geometry and partition: the parent's fit for the Hand, the Foot and the Head, and the appendage's own fit for the Arm and the Leg. Exactly one of the two fits must carry an entry for the strip, or the fit stops. The record holds:

- the trim's boundary points: the separated root loop's Gauss nodes (Hand, Foot, Leg), the armscye boundary's crossings of the trunk rows (Arm), or the outer boundary vertices of a strip region the part defines, where the appendage's skin before its first key is no root loop's shadow (the auricle's region inside the ear outline, which holds the concha, the tragus, the antitragus and the sulcus, Head);
- each point's loop parameter, or the appendage's bone line from which the strip method derives it once the appendage is fitted (the Hand, the Arm and the Leg write the line, the Foot the parameters): the appendage's phase on the point's meridian. The half-plane through the appendage's bone line that holds the point meets the appendage's fitted first-key ring at one point, whose phase it takes. This is Coons's construction between two boundary curves under a common parameter (Coons 1967, Surfaces for computer-aided design of space forms), the correspondence being the pencil of half-planes through the bone. A boundary that does not turn monotonically about the bone line stops the fit. Where no line gives a pencil both curves turn about (the auricle's first key ring, a thin flap whose area centroid lies outside it, Head), each boundary point takes its phase on its own closed boundary curve, seen along the appendage's axis, by the chain's correspondence rule (`AutoMovieHumanLoftParameter.ts:1`-`:30`), the rule that gives the first key its own phases and that the generator uses between a ring and the points it inherits. The strip pairs each point with the first key's point of the same phase. The boundary curve may be a root loop or a region's outer edge (the auricle's ear-outline region);
- the appendage's first key, against which the band is tested;
- the strip's observations with their surface-element weights (Observations and weights).

Trim: each boundary point is mapped onto the fitted parent by point inversion (Foot points), its orthogonal foot, which stays well conditioned where the bone's lines graze the skin (the leg's median 4.54 / 4.93 degrees on f1, Leg).

- The feet are fitted by a periodic cubic B-spline of M = N = 16 control points in the parent's chart, at the points' loop parameters. A Coons patch between two boundary curves needs them compatible, of one degree and one knot vector (Piegl and Tiller 1997, sec. 10.3, compatibility of curves), so the trim takes the control count of the first-key ring it is paired with. No count is searched.
- The fit is least squares in the parent's chart, each point's residual measured through the parent's first fundamental form at its foot (exact jets), so station and phase enter in millimetres of skin. It has no penalty and no smoothing parameter. A non-positive pivot stops it and names the control whose loop interval holds no point.
- This replaces the line intersection along the root plane's normal for every strip, the Hand's digit strips and the Head's auricle strips included, so one rule serves all parts.

Strip: the Hermite blend of the two surfaces' jets across the band, with no interior rows (Surface composition, Transition strips). Its one parameter, the reach, scales the cross-boundary derivatives. It is sized by Brent's minimisation (Brent 1973) of the strip's weighted squared distances to its observations, bracketed from 1 (the straight blend) and the golden ratio by golden-ratio expansion (Press et al. 2007, sec. 10.1). On each trial strip, each observation's foot is projected again by point inversion, starting from its foot on the straight blend.

It reports each column's band width, the chord between the column's two edges. A column whose trim point lies past the appendage's first key plane on the appendage's side has a crossed band, and the fit stops on it.

Two trim regions of one base that overlap stop the fit, since a junction joins two surfaces only (Surface composition). This holds on every base: the trunk's arm and leg trims, the palm's digits, the foot's toes, the head's auricles. The fit reports each base's trims' least distance apart and its cap pole's position against them (the two leg root loops come within 2.67 mm on f1, with the crotch between them, Leg).

A strip whose partition gives it no observation keeps the reach 1, the straight blend, and the fit reports it. Every strip the parts plan has its family's planes before its first key, from its root or, for the auricle, from the family's first plane, as observations, so on f1 none is expected to be empty.

### Foot points

Each node's foot `(s, t)` is its orthogonal projection on a surface, by point inversion (Piegl and Tiller 1997, The NURBS Book, sec. 6.1). The surface distance, the trims, the reliefs, the strips and the margins use it; no key is sized through it.

1. Start at the nearest point of the surface sampled at its knots and the midpoints between them, in station and in phase.
2. Newton's method on `|S(s, t) - q|^2 / 2` with the exact jets. The phase is periodic and wraps; the station's range is open at its ends, so a step past an end is set back onto that end, as Piegl and Tiller do for a parameter of an open curve or surface. On an end, a step that would leave it moves the phase alone, Newton's method in `t` with the station held.
3. Stop when the point passes step 4's zero-cosine test, Piegl and Tiller's own stopping test, or when a step is no shorter than the one before it: rounding is reached. Once quadratic convergence has reached rounding the steps are rounding noise, which shrinks or grows by chance, so the cosine test, not the step alone, ends a converged run (on a surface of f1's big toe the steps of every unconverged node fell as 1, 1e-2, 1e-5, 1e-12, then wandered at 1e-14: `node --max-old-space-size=4096 --experimental-transform-types --no-warnings --import ./.shots/claude-scratch/hooks.ts .shots/claude-scratch/method/diagnoseFeet.ts test/.shots/f1 bigtoe_l .shots/claude-scratch/method/diagnose-feet-bigtoe_l.txt`, output that file).
4. The foot is kept when it is a foot. That is Piegl and Tiller's zero-cosine test: the residual `S - q` is orthogonal to both tangents, `|S_s . (S - q)| <= sqrt(eps) |S_s| |S - q|` and the same for `S_t`, where `sqrt(eps)` is the rounding of a computed cosine. Piegl and Tiller's distance test is not used, so no distance decides a foot and a far observation keeps its true foot. On a station end the foot is the nearest point of the end's ring: it passes the test in `S_t` alone, and the distance falls only beyond the end (`S_s . (S - q) >= 0` at the first end, `<= 0` at the last, the minimum's condition on a bound: Nocedal and Wright 2006, sec. 12.3), so an end key's own observations, which rounding puts on either side of its plane, keep their feet. A foot is dropped and counted when it fails the test, or when it ends where the Hessian (in `t` alone on an end) is not positive definite.
5. At most `ceil(log2 53) + 1 = 7` iterations: Newton's convergence is quadratic near a solution, doubling the correct bits per step, so 6 steps take one correct bit to binary64's 53 (Dennis and Schnabel 1996, Numerical Methods for Unconstrained Optimization and Nonlinear Equations, ch. 5; IEEE 754-2019). A node that has not converged by then started outside Newton's quadratic region; it is dropped and counted.

The same rule projects a node along a relief's axis (`B(s, t) + h a = q`, three unknowns), maps a trim's boundary points onto their parent, and projects onto a strip. The strip has no jets, so its derivatives are central differences at the optimal steps `eps^(1/3)` and `eps^(1/4)` of the variable's scale, `eps = 2^-52` (Press et al. 2007, Numerical Recipes, 3rd ed., sec. 5.7).

Every fit reports its counts of kept and dropped nodes by reason.

### Reliefs

A relief (`HumanReliefFitting.ts`) is a height field over its fitted base, along its axis (Representation). Its part gives its window and region from landmarks and courses (Observations and weights). It also names the anatomical landmarks across the window: the stations of its apex, crest or fold lines in station, and their phases in phase.

- Net: one interior control row per named landmark station and one interior column per named landmark phase, at least one of each, uniform over the window. The three zero rings on each side make the height and its first two derivatives vanish at the window's edge, so the skin stays C2 with the base. One control per anatomical fact in each direction is the fewest that lets each named landmark carry its own height. A single landmark gives the compactly supported C2 bump of Surface composition, centred in its window.
- Heights: each node of the relief's region whose foot along the axis (Foot points) falls in the window gives its height over the base. The interior heights minimise the weighted squared height differences, each node at its surface element (Observations and weights). The fit has no penalty and no smoothing parameter. Dense Cholesky solves it (Solves), and a non-positive pivot stops the fit, naming the control whose support holds no node. A node whose foot falls outside the window is dropped and counted.
- Axis: the minimum-norm `a` with `n_i . a >= 1` over the fitted relief's normals at its nodes' feet, normalised. This is the axis of the cone of normals (Shirman and Abi-Ezzi 1993, The cone of normals technique for fast processing of curved patches), solved exactly by the dual active-set method (Goldfarb and Idnani 1983, A numerically stable dual method for solving strictly convex quadratic programs). The first fit takes the base's mean normal over the window. Axis and fit alternate until the active set repeats, and a repeat of an older set keeps the fit of lowest objective in that cycle. No feasible `a` means the feature is no height field, and the part makes it an appendage.

### Opening margins

An opening margin (`HumanMarginFitting.ts`) is a Fourier series of H harmonics per coordinate in the opening's frame (Representation). Its part names the anatomical landmarks on the margin: for an eye, the two canthi and the lids' extremes.

- Harmonics: H = L, the number of named landmarks. A trigonometric polynomial of degree H has `2H + 1` coefficients per coordinate. Giving each landmark its own position and tangent direction takes `2L` conditions, and H = L is the least degree with `2H + 1 >= 2L` (trigonometric Hermite interpolation: Zygmund 2002, Trigonometric Series, 3rd ed., ch. X).
- Fit: the coefficients minimise the weighted squared distances of the margin's observation points to the curve, the points the part names (landmark vertices or a boundary loop's Gauss nodes). Each point's parameter starts at its chord-length position round the margin and moves to its foot by Newton's method (the foot rule, Foot points): parameter correction (Hoschek 1988, Intrinsic parametrization for approximation). Fit and correction alternate until the objective falls by no more than `eps` times its value, where the objective is flat to rounding (Press et al. 2007, sec. 10.1). The fit has no penalty and no smoothing parameter.

### Solves

Every linear fit above is a small dense least-squares system: a key's 16 controls, a trim's `2M`, a relief's interior net and a margin's `2H + 1` per coordinate. Each is solved by Cholesky factorisation of its normal matrix (Golub and Van Loan 2013, Matrix Computations, 4th ed., sec. 4.2). Cholesky succeeds exactly when the matrix is positive definite, so a non-positive pivot stops the fit and names the unknown that no observation reaches. No pivot or divisor is floored.

- The reach is sized by Brent's minimisation (Brent 1973, Algorithms for Minimization without Derivatives, ch. 5) at relative tolerance `sqrt(eps)`, the least advisable.
- The relief axis is solved by the dual active-set method.
- No fit has a smoothing parameter, a cross-validation, a count search or a fixed-point loop over a whole surface.

### Viewer judgement

The viewer judges the form of every module the method builds (the owner's orders 4 and 2026-10-28 11:3x).

- Right after its fit, a part exports the module to the module-fit preview (Integration, Module-fit preview) and captures it by HTTP as `.agents/skills/human/screenshot.md` gives:
  - alone and in place;
  - in `material=clay` under both raking lights;
  - with its lofting curves in the `construction` and `overlay` passes: keys, axis and meridians;
  - in the curve diagram, with the curvature combs of its key sections and of meridians at fixed phase;
  - against the reference photographs of its region.
- A ripple, shelf, crease, flat or missing mass seen there is traced to the key, section, cap, relief or join that makes it, and is fixed by cause in this plan. It is never fixed by a count, a constant or a key added for it.
- Each view is recorded with its URL, its PNG path and its judgement.

### Surface distance

After the final fit, every node of the module, kept or not, is projected on the final composed surface (reliefs included) by the foot rule: the nodes of every observed row, not only the keys' sections. The fit's JSON gets a `distance` record: nodes observed, kept, dropped by reason, the area-weighted RMS distance, the median, 95th and 99th percentiles and the maximum of the kept distances with the maximum's point, and the same per section. Add `reportDistances.ts`, which reads every fit JSON in `.shots/claude-scratch/` and writes one table to `.shots/claude-scratch/distances.txt`; parts and Integration quote it with that command and file. The record is a report: no count, parameter, stop or acceptance reads it.

### Constants

| Constant | Value | Source |
| --- | --- | --- |
| key stations | the module's declared joints, its part's named measurement sites, its ends, `cap.from` and its cap keys | ISO 8559-1:2017; ANSUR II (Gordon et al. 2014); Farkas 1994; the bind skeleton |
| section control count N | 16 on every key of every module | four anatomical meridians and four quadrants, each with a control of its own support; cubic B-spline local support (de Boor 1978, ch. IX) |
| trim control count M | N | compatible boundary curves of a Coons strip (Piegl and Tiller 1997, sec. 10.3) |
| relief net | one interior row per named landmark station, one column per named landmark phase, at least one each, and three zero rings each side | one control per anatomical fact; C2 vanishing at the window's edge |
| margin harmonics H | L, the named landmarks on the margin | position and tangent at each landmark, `2H + 1 >= 2L` (Zygmund 2002, ch. X) |
| strip interior rows | none | the Hermite blend of the two jets (Surface composition) |
| Gauss-Legendre nodes per span | 4 | exact for degree 7 (Davis and Rabinowitz 1984) |
| foot test | zero cosine within `sqrt(eps)`; no distance test | Piegl and Tiller 1997, sec. 6.1; rounding of a computed cosine |
| margin iteration stop | objective decrease at most `eps` times its value | Press et al. 2007, sec. 10.1 |
| Newton iterations | 7 | `ceil(log2 53) + 1`, quadratic convergence (Dennis and Schnabel 1996), binary64 (IEEE 754-2019) |
| difference steps on strips | `eps^(1/3)`, `eps^(1/4)` | Press et al. 2007, sec. 5.7 |
| Brent tolerance | `sqrt(eps)` | Brent 1973 |
| cap keys | `round(rho pi / (2 D))`, at least two, the last the pole | the base's mean key spacing `D`; one key inside the closure at least, so an observed section shapes it |
| reach bracket | 1 and the golden ratio | the straight blend; Press et al. 2007, sec. 10.1 |

### Module data format

The exporter, Integration's (its section, Export), writes `IAutoMovieHumanBodyRegion` literals:

- `networks`: `IAutoMovieHumanFeatureChain` with its `anchor`, its keys, pole and relief axes in the anchor's frame (Module frames and handles), reliefs inside their chains, `ends` naming each shared key's owner, and `cap` with its pole;
- `appendages`: `IAppendage`;
- lengths to 0.001 mm and unitless values to 1e-6, as now.

The trunk region declares `stature`, the f1 specimen's applied height from `packages/playground/src/human-parameters/f1.ts`. The head region writes `apertures` and `eyes` when the Head plan places them there (`IAutoMovieHumanBodyRegion.ts:55`, `:58`). An opening stores its margin curve and each eye its globe, in its child frame in the head base's anchor coordinates, with `chain: "head"`. The generator projects an opening with a `base` onto its base as its trim (Integration, Replacement and deletions), so openings and eyes move with their modules (Head).

Shared keys are not written as events: `merge` derives them. A sharer's shared key is written as its owner's, so the literal agrees with what `compose` copies.

A chain's `samples` is the renderer's ring tessellation, not a fitted value: the fitters keep writing `2N` and Integration sets it from the render.

### Generator evaluation

Change `AutoMovieHumanBodyRegions.merge` (`generation/AutoMovieHumanBodyRegions.ts`):

1. Compose every region's networks in one `AutoMovieHumanFeatureChain.compose` call, so owners in other regions resolve and every chain carries jets; drop `expand` there. `compose` places each anchored chain's keys, pole and relief axes by its anchor before solving, and copies an owner's placed end layer into the sharer's slot exactly, without re-placing it. The child's end layer then equals the parent's in every bit, which the Integration section's inherited-phase rule tests.
2. For every shared end, add the continuation event `{parent: owner, direction: owner end "top" ? "up" : "down", splits: [{child, arcs: [[0, 2 pi]]}]}`, so the sharer inherits its owner's ring instead of closing at a pole (`AutoMovieHumanBodySurface.boundary`).

Strips, trims and shading normals then read exact jets everywhere.

### Files

Generator, `packages/human/src` (Method, step 3):

- `network/AutoMovieHumanFeatureChain.ts`: `compose` places an anchored chain by its anchor and copies a shared end layer exactly. It also takes an all-cap module, whose only key before the cap is `cap.from` (Caps): that key's plane slope and curvature come from the cap formula at `cap.from`, since the base's plane spline needs two keys (`derivatives` reads `t[lo + 1] - t[lo]` with `lo = min(i, n - 2)`, `:158`-`:176`, `:240`-`:250`). `weights`, `IWeights` and `IOwnerWeights`, which served only the erased regression, are deleted. `compose` carries the anchor, the named axes and the declared joints, placed in the world, onto each loft chain, so the merged calibration hands them to the consumers (Integration, "Frames, anchors and joints").
- `lofting/IAutoMovieHumanLoftChain.ts`: the placed anchor, axes and joints a composed chain carries.
- `network/IAutoMovieHumanFeatureChain.ts`: add `anchor`. For an anchored chain, require `frame` on every key and read stations, key frames and the pole in the anchor's frame; the JSDoc states it.
- `network/IAutoMovieHumanRelief.ts`: `axis` in the base chain's anchor frame when the base is anchored.
- `lofting/IAutoMovieHumanLoftAperture.ts` and `generation/IAutoMovieHumanBodyCalibration.ts` (`IEye`): `chain` and `frame`, and `merge` places them by that chain's anchor. The consumers' use of the frame (the outline's axes in `AutoMovieHumanLoftAperture.ts`, the eyelid's up, the appearance eyes) is Integration's (Replacement and deletions, 10).
- `generation/AutoMovieHumanBodyRegions.ts`: `merge` as above.

Shared fitting code, `test/src/executable/reference-experiments/human-calibration/` (Method, step 3):

- `HumanModuleFitting.ts`: the driver of a chain. It takes the module's geometry with its key stations, sizes every key from its own section (`HumanModuleKeys.section`), adds the cap keys and the held pole key, copies the owners' shared end layers, tests every pair of keys for admissibility, and writes the chain and the distance record. Deleted with the erased regression: `sdm`, `sdmEnd`, `designs`, `penalties`, `basis`, `toBasis`, `fromBasis`, `moved`, `residual`, `unknowns`, `update`, `trace`, `limits`, `nearestPoints`, the admissible interval, the fixed-parameter loop with its Armijo safeguard, the performance iteration, `select` with the coordinate search, `dataLimits`, `ICounts`, and from `IFit` the selection, performance, alternation, backtrack, limit and interval records. The part fitters' region code maps landmarks and root loops to the chart by the foot rule instead of `sampling` and `project`.

It also holds a part's partition: per-node labels from the part's predicates, rows cut into arcs at named planes by intersection, the partition record, and the drop reasons with "relief region outside its window" and "plane tangent to the skin".
- `HumanModuleAnchor.ts` (new): a module's anchor from its joint's bind affine through the polar decomposition, its named axes (from joints, a carried parent axis or landmarks), named frames and declared joints in the anchor's coordinates, a child frame in its parent anchor's coordinates, and the rigid conversion of keys, poles and relief axes between the world and the anchor's frame.

## Retirement

What is deleted, in this order within step 3:

1. Method lands the shared fitting code, the anchor fields, the apertures' and eyes' `chain` and `frame`, and `merge`, and deletes `weights`. The new fields are optional, as `anchor` is, so the world-height literals compile until step 3 deletes them; the exporter writes them on every module, opening and eye. The world-height calibration still renders, since no region carries networks yet. Method then runs the observation tools in the order of Observation families: the `-v3` digit and toe families with every `-root-v3.json` (the arms' and legs' among them), the digits' and toes' terminals, the head and auricle families, the end-plane terminals of the palms, feet, trunk and head base from the planes their parts name, and every module's key planes from the stations its part names. The outputs are private under `test/.shots/`.
2. The parts fit in the skill's order (Trunk, then Head; Hand, then Arm; Foot, then Leg; strips after both their modules), each module viewed in the module-fit preview right after its fit (Viewer judgement). No fitter reads the world-height calibration:
   - `fitTrunkBase.ts` sizes its keys from the trunk's own sections at its anatomical stations, the neck ring on the Neck Base course's plane (Trunk);
   - `fitHead.ts` sizes its keys from the head's own sections at its anatomical stations, in its own axis's plane function, and fits the openings and eyes from the landmarks and the mesh (Head);
   - every pole is its module's terminal.
3. Integration exports the modules and replaces `AutoMovieHumanBodyCalibration` by the merge of the module regions, the head region carrying the eyes and every opening, with no repair rule. In the same change it deletes:
   - `calibration/AutoMovieHumanTorsoCalibration.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanFootCalibration.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanEarCalibration.ts` and `AutoMovieHumanEyeCalibration.ts`, whose nostril openings, pockets, eye openings and globes the head region carries (Head; Integration, Replacement and deletions);
   - `AutoMovieHumanSectionLoops.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanContactSmoothing.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanLipSmoothing.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanCornerSmoothing.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanStationContinuity.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanEventJoin.ts`;
   - in `AutoMovieHumanBodySurface.ts`: `ringBlend` and its constants, the 30-degree fallback of `surfaceNormals` (every chain carries jets), `domeBase`, the dome in `pole` and their constants;
   - the shape basis and the warp, which read world-height chain names and layers and cannot carry over (survey root cause 5): `calibration/AutoMovieHumanBodyMorphCalibration.ts`, `IAutoMovieHumanBodyMorph.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanBodyWarpField.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanBodyWarp.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanBodyGirth.ts`, `AutoMovieHumanBodyLandmarks.ts`, and `fitBodyMorph.ts`; `AutoMovieHumanBodyLaw.calibrate` returns the module calibration and `AutoMovieHumanVermilionCalibration` for every input until #2998 builds the measurement law, so f2, m1 and m2 render as f1 scaled to their stature until then. `calibration/AutoMovieHumanBodySpecimenCalibration.ts` (f1's applied inputs, no chain) stays for that law.
4. Integration re-points every consumer of world-height chain names (survey, Couplings and "Everything that depends on it") to the module chains, then renders f1. It brings the documents that describe the retired code to the current facts in the same change: `docs/techniques/section-chain-lofting.md` (the shape basis) and the fitters' `README.md` row for `fitBodyMorph.ts`.

No calibration stays in world coordinates (Order 2). `AutoMovieHumanEyeCalibration` stored apertures and globes in the world with world up, so the head region replaces it with openings and eyes in child frames of the head anchor (Head, Eye openings). `AutoMovieHumanVermilionCalibration` keeps its saved Recovery spans, now written in the head base's anchor coordinates with `chain: "head"` by the exporter (Head; Integration, Export 2).

| --- | --- |

| root cause 5 (basis and law tied to chain names) | Retirement step 3 deletes them; #2998 builds the law on the modules |

### Requests from Method

The reorganised shared method changes what each part's section must state. Every part re-aligns its section to it:

1. Key stations: list every module's anatomical stations. These are the declared bind joints in its station range and the measurement sites from ISO 8559-1, ANSUR II or Farkas, each with its definition and, for an extreme, the observed sections it is taken over. Drop every key count, data limit, count search, admissible interval and start-key rule. A long segment with no site between two keys shows in the viewer as missing mass. Method's exercise of f1's left leg had only the knee and ANSUR II's calf and ankle circumferences, and its thigh lost its fullness between the first key and the knee (Self-review, "Shared method, views", v15 to v17). The Leg names its thigh sites, and every part checks its long segments the same way.
2. Sections: N = 16 on every module. Drop each N selection, each shared-N selection over an owner and its sharers, and every reference to held-out scores, folds, smoothing parameters, penalties or the one-standard-error rule.
3. Key planes: give the stations so that Method observes the key planes (Observation families, Key planes). The part labels each key section's nodes by the same partition predicates as its family's.
4. Caps: an all-cap module (an auricle) names its cap keys' stations along the cap's meridian.
5. Reliefs: name each relief's landmark stations and phases across its window, which fix its net. Drop the net count search and the relief smoothing.
6. Strips: drop the interior rows, the trim's M search and the strip's blocks; M = N, and the record's observations carry surface-element weights.
7. Opening margins: name each margin's landmarks, which fix H = L. Drop the harmonic search and the margin smoothing.
8. Viewer: record, for each module, the views of Viewer judgement with their URLs, PNG paths and judgements.

## Trunk

The Trunk part fits one trunk base and four reliefs, the two breasts and the two buttocks, by the shared method, and owns the neck ring. Every module lives in the trunk's own frame, anchored at the pelvis joint's bind frame (Module frames and handles), with its axis and key frames derived from the spine and hip joints, and is placed by that frame, the body courses and the root loops (Order 2): no world height, absolute coordinate or person-indexed value defines a key, an end or a region, and the f1 numbers below only illustrate; its counts and smoothing come from the shared selection, so nothing below adds a key, raises a count or carves a region for a row the surface misses, and the surface distance is a report no Trunk rule reads. Survey facts are in the survey's "Trunk" subsection, with the commands that printed them.

### Trunk base

`trunk`, rule `sagittal`, the chain name the consumers read.

- Axis: the natural cubic spline through the bind joints `pelvis`, `spine_01` to `spine_05`, `neck_01` and `neck_02`, parameterised by chord length (the interpolant of least `integral |r''|^2` through the joints: de Boor 1978, A Practical Guide to Splines, ch. IV), continued below the pelvis by its end tangent, the straight line its zero end curvature gives. The anchor is the pelvis joint, the skeleton's root, as the shared method gives it; a station is arc length along the axis from it, negative below it, and a point's station is that of its foot on the axis. A polyline of the joints would turn the normal planes by a jump at every joint, so the trunk's axis is the spline through them, as Module frames and handles allows a part's own curve through the same joints.
- Frame: the named axes at the pelvis are built from joint centres: along, the axis tangent t; front, `t x h`, h the hip axis (`thigh_l` minus `thigh_r`), the body's forward; lateral, along x front, as the shared Interface's named axes define it, so it points along -h, to the body's right. The key frames follow the shared Representation rule: at the first key, u is the forward axis made normal to the key's plane and v completes it so that v x u is the plane's normal, carried by double-reflection rotation-minimising transport (Wang, Juttler, Zheng and Liu 2008), as the limbs carry theirs. The transport runs along the axis on a grid halved until a halving no longer shrinks the change of the end frames (rounding), so every key's frame is the axis's rotation-minimising frame at its station whichever keys the selection takes, as the shared driver reads a module's frame as a function of station; the survey's measure carries the same frame from the pelvis. v therefore points along h, toward the skeleton's `_l` joints. An angle runs about the axis from u toward v, so the angles 0 and ±180 are the frame's sagittal plane, which on f1 holds every point of the Rise and Neck to Waist courses (survey).
- Key planes: a key lies on the axis's normal plane at its station, in the carried frame. It need not be an observed row's plane: the fit projects every node onto the surface, and the horizontal rows stay observations only. A curve's normal planes meet their neighbours along its polar line, at the radius of curvature along the principal normal (Struik 1961, Lectures on Classical Differential Geometry), so they cross inside the skin only where the skin reaches past it. On f1 the skin stays at least 7.9 mm inside it (survey); the fitter checks the same on every person and stops where it fails.
- Bottom end: the trunk's terminal is a saddle, so `cap.from` is the last station whose section is wholly the trunk's own skin (shared Caps): the largest station of the leg root loops' points (-101.07 mm on f1), so the end key's plane lies on the trunk's side of every leg root point. The pole is the shared Caps rule's terminal, the crotch saddle where the trunk's component splits into the legs: the `cap.from` plane is observed on its own with its normal reversed, and its loop round the axis seeds Recovery's terminal (Observation families). `cap.from` is read from the legs' `-root-v3.json` loops, so within Retirement step 1 Method observes the trunk's terminal after the legs' roots. The terminal is not yet observed; on f1 the axis, continued below the pelvis, meets the Rise course at station -112.67 mm and the course's lowest point lies at -113.35 mm (survey). `rho`, the cap keys and their planes follow the shared Caps rule; the trunk's side of the rows below `cap.from` reaches into the cap and shapes it.
- Top end: the neck ring, the key on the plane of the Neck Base course, the anthropometric course round the base of the neck. The course is planar, its points within 0.00 mm of their orthogonal least-squares plane on f1, and the plane crosses each trunk row it meets twice, round the neck (survey); the plane is the course's least-squares plane on every person (the eigenvector of the least eigenvalue of the points' covariance: Pearson 1901, On lines and planes of closest fit to systems of points in space). The key's station is where the axis crosses that plane (537.86 mm on f1). Its axes are the carried frame turned by the least rotation that takes t onto the plane's normal (18.4 degrees on f1), about `t x n`. It is a free key of the trunk fit; the trunk has no `ends` entry. The head base holds a copy (`ends.bottom = {owner: "trunk", end: "top"}`) with its frame and the common N (N below), and `compose` gives the head's first interval the trunk's end slope and curvature. The trunk's natural-spline end condition at its last key (zero second station derivative) is therefore also the head's at the ring. Skin on the trunk's side of the plane is the trunk's, beyond it the head's.
- Keys: the selected number at evenly spaced admissible stations between `cap.from` and the neck ring, never moved to a row (Keys and control counts). A station is admissible by the shared rule: the line its normal plane shares with each end key's plane misses the inside of the end key's loop and of the station's own start loop. The survey measured the same against the trunk rows' nodes near each station: on f1, 28 whole-millimetre stations between 509.00 and 537.00 mm meet the neck base plane (survey). No key sits at a measurement site; #2998 keeps the sites named.
- Storage: as Module frames and handles stores every anchored chain: each key's station, its frame and the pole in the pelvis anchor's coordinates, the coefficients in the key's frame. A later transform moves the anchor, or the joints the axis is built from, and the same rules re-derive the frames; no key is tied to a world height.
